# BUILD PROGRESS

> **Cursor: you must update this file at the end of every slice, before finishing your turn.**
> **Human: upload this file to a new Claude chat to restore full context.**

**Project:** English Learning System — web app (PWA), 2 users, B1 → B2 in 6 months. Telegram is a notification channel, not the product.
**Repo:** `english-bot`
**Last updated:** 2026-08-24
**Current slice:** W4b
**Status:** **v2 is closed** — the Telegram bot build ended at S26c. The **v3 web rebuild** (`docs/TASKS-v3-web.md`) is at **W4b: deployed to production on 2026-08-24 and 🟡, awaiting the human's mark.** **Identity is decoupled from Telegram.** Migration **011** is live (`Applied: 001–011, Pending: (none)`): `users.id` is a surrogate primary key, `telegram_user_id` is nullable and UNIQUE, all 17 user foreign keys point at `users(id)`, and **a person can now exist in this database without a Telegram account** — `PRODUCT-PRINCIPLES.md` §2 satisfied by the schema for the first time. **#92 is closed**, verified on production by a query run separately from the migration's own assertions: ids 1 / 2 / 3 with every Telegram id preserved, errors 0 + 26 + 7 = 33, `user_lexemes` 6,075, both totals identical to before. The already-signed-in phone opened with **no re-enrolment prompt**. Suite **1103 passing / 0 failing**. **W5 is next, and it is now migration 012** — W4b took 011 and every unwritten slice shifted by one. **The lexicon is seeded on production and the ledger is repaired.** **W4 deployed to production on 2026-08-24 and the deploy found a fault in it** — the conflict rule gated demotion on source rank, so passive exposure demoted assumed-known words and **coverage fell as a learner used the app more** (231 of 2,000 floor lemmas for the learner with the most v2 history; **#91**). W4a gates demotion on evidence of not-knowing (`MAY_LOWER`) instead of authority, and its repair **ran on production on 2026-08-24: 245 floor rows restored** (234 + 11 + 0), coverage 1769 → **2003** for the learner with the most v2 history, and a second run wrote nothing. **#91 is closed.** **No migration** — 011 belongs to W5. **Both W4 and W4a stay 🟡 until the human marks them.** Suite **1068 passing / 0 failing**. W4 itself deployed cleanly: backup 68,586 bytes to R2, `010` applied, `Applied: 001–010, Pending: (none)`, seed idempotent on the real database, and **no `source='capture'` warning** — an evidenced negative saying the error journal holds no rows that should not be there. W4 lays the foundation the whole knowledge model stands on: migration **010** (`lexemes`, `user_lexemes`), a 15,000-lemma frequency + CEFR seed in `data/`, and a pure text → %-known-coverage function. The lemmatiser is a **committed lookup table generated offline**, so the runtime gains no NLP dependency and the settled deployment sequence gains no step. **The licence verification gate ran first and caught the plan's own table wrong in both rows** — FrequencyWords is CC BY-SA 4.0 for content rather than MIT, and CEFR-J is a TUFS permission rather than a Creative Commons licence; `data/LICENCES.md` carries the corrected terms verbatim and **#87** records the redistribution gap. Suite **974 → 1068 passing / 0 failing (1068 collected)**. **W3 was deployed to production on 2026-08-24 and marked ✅.** W3 gives the web app its first teaching surface — `POST /correct` over the shared correction service and a "Write anything" screen — and moved the correction logic out of `apps/bot/handlers/` into `packages/core/services/correction.py`. The move is **confirmed live by the bot's own startup log**, which now reads `INFO core.services.correction: Loaded 19 error types for correction prompt` where it read `apps.bot.handlers.correction` before. **#45 is closed** — the single-language / no-transliteration rule is one constant in all five explanation paths, verified with a real API call per template. **No migration in the slice:** production reports `schema_version: 9`, which is W2's 009 and unchanged. Suite **974 passing / 0 failing (974 collected)**. **W1c and W2 are ✅ (marked 2026-08-24); W2a stays 🟡** — its fix to the false connectivity banner ships in this restart, and only the human marks it. W1c's off-site backup to Cloudflare R2 is now proven end to end and **#6 is closed**; the restore-drill numbers, the byte counts and the R2 mechanics live in #6, #70, #73 and `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md`, not here. `english-bot` and `english-api` both run; `english-worker` is still deliberately uninstalled (#69). Open and carried: **#65** (no operator alert channel — now with a money-spending route behind it, and the highest-priority open infrastructure issue), the new **#82** (the v3 documents describe the intended structure, not the actual one — W4 must plan from the repo tree), #44, #67, #70, #72, #73, #79, #80, #81 and the v2 set. **New from the 2026-08-24 Telegram evidence:** **#83** (two hosts can poll one bot token — high; the only open issue that can write learner data to the wrong database), #84, #85 and #86. **New from W4:** **#87** (CEFR-J redistribution gap — medium, crystallises at a sale or an open-sourcing), **#88** (the coverage band contradicts itself across PRD and TASKS), **#89** and **#90**.

**W1c and W2 are ✅ (marked 2026-08-24).** `app.foundgrant.com` serves over HTTPS; `api.foundgrant.com` has a Let's Encrypt certificate through Caddy; migration **009 is applied to production**; `english-api` runs two uvicorn workers on `127.0.0.1:8000`; **both learners are enrolled**, and the production access log carries the **full WebAuthn registration ceremony** (`register/begin` → `register/finish` → `health/auth`, all 200, 20:33:41–47). `fonderis-worker` was verified answering after the Caddy reload, and a hostile CORS preflight was correctly refused with 400. The API is **scanned continuously** from the moment its certificate issued — all 404, nothing exposed — which makes **#65 (no operator alert channel) the highest-priority open infrastructure issue**, to fix before or with W3. **W2a** (🟡) fixed the one user-facing bug the deploy exposed: a working API reported as unreachable. Suite at W2a: **926 passing / 0 failing**; **974 / 0 (974 collected)** after W3. **W4 and W4a are both deployed to production (2026-08-24).** Suite **974 → 1068 passing / 0 failing (1068 collected)**. The W4 deploy found a fault in W4 — passive exposure demoted assumed-known words, so coverage fell as a learner used the app more — and W4a closed it: **245 floor rows restored (234 + 11 + 0), coverage 1769 → 2003 for the learner with the most v2 history, second run wrote nothing, confirmed by an independent `psql` query. #91 is closed.** **The lexicon is seeded on production and the ledger is repaired; nothing reads coverage until W12.** **Next is the hand-checked coverage number** — a paragraph the human has actually read, run through `coverage_for`, judged against how hard it felt; no test can substitute for it. **W4b then re-keyed the whole schema** — migration 011, live on production 2026-08-24, both learners verified intact, **#92 closed**. **W4, W4a and W4b all stay 🟡;** only the human marks any of them. Unrun: `/stats` on the restructured bot (`/ping` answered `pong` on 2026-08-24). **The phone `/write` check ran on 2026-08-24 and is off the list.** Every v2 desk check remains unrun — infrastructure and a re-key exercise no learner path.

## Decisions log

Record every decision that deviates from or resolves ambiguity in the spec. Newest first.

| Date | Decision | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| 2026-08-25 | **W5b: #115's cause is the INPUT to the judge, not the judge and not the fixtures.** The gate hands `judge_naturalness` a string containing `___`, or one containing a deliberate grammatical error, and then asks a model whether a real person would say it. Confirmed by a zero-cost dry run over the committed fixtures: the four rejected types are exactly the four whose judged string is defective, and the six that passed are exactly the six whose judged string is prose. Eleven of eleven, no exceptions. | **Both of #115's branches were wrong, which is why neither could be chosen.** The judge is not over-rejecting — a model told to reject what *"nobody actually phrases that way"* is **correct** to reject a sentence with a blank in it. And `valid.json` is not invalid — the items are ordinary B1 English, which is why they read that way to a person. The defect is in `sentence_of`'s contract with the judge, which is a third possibility the issue did not contain. **This also forecloses the fix everyone would reach for first**: there is no threshold, no score and no constant — the verdict is a raw boolean — so the gate cannot be quietly loosened until the fixtures pass, and the temptation CLAUDE.md §3 rule 7 warns about has nothing to act on. |
| 2026-08-25 | **W5b: the counterfactual is NOT claimed, and the slice says so.** The code proves what the judge was asked. It does not prove what the judge would say to the same sentence with the gap closed. That single question is arm B, it is unrun, and the record states the finding as *cause identified, counterfactual outstanding* rather than as settled. | The partition is strong enough that the conclusion is very likely, and *very likely* is not *measured*. The whole reason #115 exists is that a run produced a number without the evidence to interpret it; writing an unrun inference into the record as a result would be the same fault wearing better clothes. **#115 is updated with its cause and deliberately not closed** — it closes when the fix lands, not when the explanation is convincing. |
| 2026-08-25 | **W5b: the judge is observed through the EXISTING `gates._chat` seam, not rebuilt.** `_recording` wraps the seam, delegates to the real callable, captures the raw response and restores it — including on the exception path, which has its own test. So the harness calls the real `judge_naturalness` and reports its real verdict. | **The alternative was reconstructing the request by hand**, and it would have been wrong for the reason W5a already paid for: the harness and the gate would drift, and the finding would describe a request production never makes. The verdict and the `reason` now come from *the same call*, which is the W5a lesson stated exactly — **the thing that verifies must measure the thing it reports on.** `arm_as_shipped` is a one-line delegation to `sentence_of` for the same reason, and a test pins the two byte-identical across all eleven fixtures so a later change to `sentence_of` breaks the suite rather than the finding. |
| 2026-08-25 | **W5b: five samples per fixture per arm, and the caveat travels with the numbers.** Arm A 10×5, arm B 4×5, arm C 2×3 — 76 calls on `claude-sonnet-5`, under $0.30. | One sample of a stochastic judge is what produced #115's uncertainty in the first place, so one more sample would have reproduced the problem rather than answered it. Five makes the primary question decisive — under "it is just noise", arm A rejecting 5/5 while arm B accepts 5/5 across all four items is ~1e-12 — and buys a **stability number for the six that passed** in the same run, which matters independently: if `dictation` comes back 3/5 rather than 5/5 then the run-to-run variance is far wider than anyone assumed. **What five samples of eleven items licenses is unchanged from `verify --live` and is printed by the harness itself**: it distinguishes a deterministic cause from a stochastic one and **nothing more**. No claim about the gate's rate in either direction, and **it is not a yield number for W10** — these fixtures were hand-written to be good and generated items will not be. |

The asymmetry that makes UNMEASURED *widen* where `< 10%` would *narrow* is deliberate and is written into `naturalness.py`: measured-low is **evidence** the sentences are clean, so widening only adds false rejections; unmeasured is **absence** of evidence, where a false reject costs one regenerated item overnight and a false accept ships a Slack-sounding sentence to a learner. |

| 2026-08-24 | **W5: `items` is per-learner fan-out, not a shared library with per-user delivery rows.** | An item is **not shareable by construction**. PRD §3: the content inside a unit is generated against your error profile, your interests and your known-word ledger. An item built from `errors.id 47` means nothing to the other learner, and the coverage gate is per-learner, which makes **validity itself user-scoped** — the same sentence can sit inside one learner's comprehensible band and outside the other's. A shared row would need a per-user validation record, which is the delivery table with extra steps, and the shared row would then hold nothing but a string. S24's lesson lands directly: every consumer (W7's FSRS, W10's builder, W11's re-queue) reads "this learner's items", and a shared table gives each of them a join they can forget. `content_hash` exists from day one because it is the key a future de-dup into a shared library would need. **PRODUCT-PRINCIPLES §3 flag:** the genuine cheap-now/expensive-later note points the *other* way — a fixed authored bank does not go in this table; W18's `placement_bank` (019) is that shape, and PRD §6 requires the instrument not to change. |

The alias is the interesting part: `BaseModel`'s metaclass is an `ABCMeta`, which already carries a `register` method, so a field of that name **parses without error and then reads back as a bound method**. It was caught in a smoke test, not by a type checker. The wire name and the column stay `register`; only Python attribute access differs, and the reason is written at the field so nobody "fixes" it back. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W5: `item_attempts` ships six columns with no reader — `latency_ms`, `cue_shown`, `chosen_option`, `grade`, `graded_by`, `audio_seconds`.** | Adding a column at 013 or 015 is one line; the months of history in between are gone permanently, and that history is exactly what seeds FSRS. `cue_shown` is the sharpest case: `items.cue_type` is the item's *current* state, and W7's leech rule rewrites an item "with an easier cue" — the moment that happens, every past attempt silently re-reads as if it had carried the new cue. `graded_by` is the second: typed items grade by string match, `speak_answer` needs a rubric, and a learner may self-mark, so without it an accuracy number mixes three instruments and W19's progress line stops being comparable — the same argument PRD §6 makes for keeping the placement instrument fixed. **The audio itself is never stored** (CLAUDE.md §5); only its duration. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W5: all six new `errors.source` values are classified against W4's harvest allow-list in this slice, and `item` is deliberately NOT harvested.** `answer` and `retell` are harvested (keyboard-authored, `text`'s class); `shadow`, `video`, `placement` and `item` are not. | W4's axis is: did the learner **type** it, or did a recogniser **guess** it? `item` is the one that needed a decision rather than a lookup, because it covers typed *and* ASR-graded responses under a single value — harvesting it would let a `speak_answer` mishearing promote a word to `known`, which is permanent damage where a missing harvest is recoverable. W5 ships `core.items.RESPONSE_MODE` (tap / typed / spoken) so W6 and W7 can split them honestly instead of widening the allow-list and hoping. `placement` is excluded for a different reason: PRD §6 requires the instrument not to change, and harvesting from it feeds the measurement back into the thing measured. The classification is machine-checked — `HARVESTED_SOURCES` / `NOT_HARVESTED_SOURCES` in `states.py` must partition the CHECK exactly, so a seventh value cannot be added without a decision. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W5: `llm.py` and `speech.py` are UNCHANGED, so CLAUDE.md §3 rule 2 does not fire — and the one human-run verification is proposed anyway.** | Rule 2 is scoped to *"any change to `llm.py` or `speech.py` request construction"*. W5 uses `system` + `json_mode` + `max_tokens` + `reject_truncation`, all already exercised by five shipped features, and the audio gate is a new **composition** of two unmodified calls rather than a new request shape. Test-suite calls are **zero structurally**, not by discipline: `netguard` is autouse and session-scoped, so a test reaching a provider raises rather than spends. `python -m core.items.verify` defaults to `--dry-run` and prints the exact request without sending it; `--live` and `--live-audio` are three calls total, human-run, and are in Next action rather than in acceptance. **What a `--live` pass licenses is written into the module**: the request shape is accepted, the projection is well-formed, and the gate *can* reject an ambiguous item — one sample of a stochastic system, no claim about the gate's rate. A fail is decisive; a pass is not proof. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W5: the PRD's own broken item passes every deterministic check and is caught only by the blind solver — and an early version got that wrong.** `looks_proper_noun` flagged `I'll` as a name, so the item was rejected for the wrong reason. | The item was rejected either way, so nothing user-visible changed. **What changed is the reason recorded in `items.validation`**, and a wrong reason there sends the next person to tune the wrong half of the generator prompt. PRD §4.3 makes the same point about the same item: "this is not a prompt-tuning problem; it is a missing validation layer." A contraction is capitalised, carries an apostrophe and cannot be resolved by the lemma table, so it tripped all three heuristics at once; the fix is to exclude contractions explicitly, and there is now a named test asserting the item survives the deterministic pass. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W5: cue material (`definition`, `l1_gloss`) is authored by the generator in the same call that writes the item, and the word bank is built from the SOLVER'S OWN WRONG ANSWERS.** | PRD's repair ladder must be deterministic — a model-chosen cue is a billed call per repair and would make the acceptance fixtures repair differently on each run. But two of the five cues need *content* that cannot be derived from the item, so the generator supplies them up front at no extra cost and unused rungs are never rendered. The word bank falls out for free and is better than anything authored in advance: PRD's own example bank is `I'll · I'd · I'm`, which is exactly what a solver produces when it fails. **The gate's failure output becomes the repair's input**, so the item ends up cued against the ambiguity actually observed rather than one imagined. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W5: `items.track` is `NOT NULL`, and the existing corpus is the argument.** | PRD §4.6 rule 2 bans jargon *unless the item's track is Work*, so the rule is conditional on this column and a NULL track would silently skip the gate — the slice's own acceptance criterion would stop being enforceable. This is not hypothetical: **24 of the 29 surviving production `chunks` carry a NULL `track`**, so 83% of them would have skipped it. A nullable column with a conditional rule attached is a rule that does not run. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W5: rejected items are never written, and there is no `status` column.** | `status IN ('ready','rejected')` would put a filter in every consumer's WHERE clause, and a filter everyone must remember is a filter someone forgets — which here means a rejected item reaching a learner. The validator returns a `ValidationReport` and acceptance asserts on the return value, never on a log line. "No item reaches a learner without a validation record" is held up by three layers instead: `validation JSONB NOT NULL` with no default plus a `? 'deterministic'` CHECK, `insert_item` refusing a report that is not `ok`, and a parse test asserting **exactly one** `INSERT INTO items` in the tree. The third is the only one that survives a refactor. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W5: `core/items/grading.fold` is a third fold, pinned by assertion rather than by an import.** | `normalize_for_match` lives in `core/services/reading.py`, a module full of SQL, and `core/lexicon/normalize.py` already records the decision not to import across that boundary — the two are pinned by a test instead. `normalize_for_match` has ~25 call sites, so moving it is a slice of its own and W5 does not widen into it. The existing pin gained one assertion covering the items fold. **This matters more here than anywhere else in the tree**: `fold` is what the blind-solver gate and the grader both use, and if it drifted, an item would pass the uniqueness gate and then be ungradable — the learner types the identical string and is marked wrong. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W5: pydantic becomes a direct dependency of `core`, and `core.lexicon` was missing from the packaging list since W4.** | The validator's core job is parsing an eleven-variant JSON payload from a model, which a discriminated union does in ~120 lines against ~400 of hand-rolled dispatch whose failure mode is a silently-ignored key. It already arrives in every venv transitively (via `anthropic`; `apps/api` imports it directly), so declaring it turns an undeclared transitive dependency into a declared one and the deploy sequence gains no step. Separately: `[tool.setuptools] packages` read `["core", "core.services"]` and **had never listed `core.lexicon`** — the editable install resolves it by path so nothing ever failed, but a wheel build would have dropped the entire W4 lexicon package. Both it and `core.items` are declared now. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4b deployed. Migration 011 is live on production, verified independently of its own assertions.** Rehearsal: `restore_from_r2.sh --keep` on `english_bot/2026/08/english_bot_2026-08-24_1425.dump` (272,751 bytes), drill passed in 3s with errors/chunks/users/sessions matching live; **011 against that scratch copy in 0.330s**. Deploy: backup 272,751 bytes to R2 (`…_1429.dump`, 14:29:48 UTC) → `systemctl stop english-bot english-api` → `Applied: 011` → `Applied: 001–011, Pending: (none)` → both services `active`. **Downtime under one minute.** Post-migration, on both the scratch copy and production: **ids 1 / 2 / 3 with every Telegram id preserved, errors 0 + 26 + 7 = 33, `user_lexemes` 2000 + 2006 + 2069 = 6,075** — both totals identical to before. | The independent `psql` check is the part worth recording, not the counts. The migration asserts its own row counts and aborts on a mismatch, which is the right place for that guard — but a migration reporting on its own success is asserting a claim. A query written separately against the same tables is a result. Same distinction W4a's repair was held to, and the same reason: #91 existed because something that looked right in 1,050 tests was wrong in the only place that mattered. **0.330s against a ~30s ceiling** is also worth keeping: the number the plan set was never approached, so the stop-and-re-plan branch was never needed. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4b: `systemctl stop english-bot english-api` before `migrate` was a slice-specific step, NOT a change to the settled deployment sequence.** The sequence remains backup → pull → `pip install -e packages/core` → migrate → restart, and it is not re-argued. | 011 holds `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` on `users` and `access_requests` for its entire transaction. Requests would not have errored, they would have **blocked** — and the sharper hazard is the lock *queue*: one bot handler holding an open transaction makes the `ALTER` wait, and every reader arriving afterwards queues behind the waiting `ALTER`, turning a sub-second migration into a stall for everything. Stopping first removed that entirely and made the window a bounded, deliberate outage rather than a hang that looks like a bug. The cost was already being paid — `apps/bot/main.py:196` is `run_polling(drop_pending_updates=True)`, so the ordinary restart at step 5 already discards anything sent while the bot is down. **This applied to W4b only. A future slice wanting it must justify it again.** |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4b: the first rehearsal attempt did not run — a pasted command carried a placeholder DSN and failed with `failed to resolve host`.** It was caught, production was confirmed at `Applied: 001–010, Pending: 011` with both services `active`, and the rehearsal was then run properly. **Nothing reached production out of order.** | Recorded because the near-miss is the interesting part, not the typo. The placeholder failed *loudly* here only because the next step checked schema state before proceeding — had the DSN resolved to something real instead of nothing, the failure would have been silent and in the wrong database. This is the same shape as #64 (a scratch `.env` being silently ignored while the real configuration loads) and the same reason CLAUDE.md §5b insists on exported variables: the reliable protection is not careful pasting, it is a check between the step and the thing it affects. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4b: the per-user assertion was quadratic, and only a numeric ceiling in the plan caught it.** `IS NOT DISTINCT FROM` cannot be hash-joined, so the check nested-loop scanned every user pair: **34s** on a scratch database carrying 31,105 `access_requests` rows of test residue, roughly 10,000× production's size. Comparing keys as TEXT with an explicit `'~NULL~'` sentinel — which cannot collide with a user id — took it to **0.55s**, and to 0.330s on the real restore. | **The general lesson is the one to keep: production's small size would have hidden this permanently.** On three users the assertion would have passed in milliseconds and stayed quadratic, waiting silently for a table to grow — and this is a migration, so it would have surfaced years later as an unexplained lock held over a live database. It was found only because the plan committed to a **number** (~30s) rather than to "should be fast", and because the build stopped at 4 seconds over it instead of shrugging. A ceiling with no number attached would not have caught this. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4b: the migration's assertion was proven to fire, not assumed.** A deliberately swapped mapping — one that preserves every per-table total, 10 errors before and 10 after — was **caught by the per-user check**, aborted the transaction, and left the database at `schema_version 10` with the data untouched. | CLAUDE.md §3 rule 4: a green check over a path nothing exercises is decoration. The specific thing worth recording is that **per-table totals alone would have passed it** — a swap conserves them exactly. Counting per user is what turns "nothing was lost" into "nothing was lost *and* nothing moved to the wrong learner", which is the failure that actually matters for an error journal. It also confirmed the transaction guarantee end to end: a failure mid-migration leaves nothing behind. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4b: the fresh-id decision paid off measurably, and the number is the argument.** Rewriting the suite surfaced **~490 failures**, nearly all foreign-key violations naming the exact table and the exact value. | Under the rejected seeded-id scheme (`users.id` seeded from `telegram_user_id`) **every one of those 490 would have passed silently**, because the two numbers would have been equal for exactly the three learners the tests use — and would have diverged only for the first Telegram-less person, months later, with no test able to catch it. This is the general argument, worth keeping past this slice: **make a type error self-detecting on the rows you actually have.** The cost was one data rewrite of small tables; the return was 490 loud failures instead of one silent one. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4b: `users` gains a surrogate key and `telegram_user_id` becomes a nullable, unique secondary identifier. The cheap alternative — minting synthetic Telegram ids for web users — is rejected permanently.** | `PRODUCT-PRINCIPLES.md` §2 requires that a person can sign up, learn and pay without Telegram, and the schema forbade it: `telegram_user_id` was the primary key and 17 foreign keys pointed at it. Synthetic ids cost nothing today and are a lie in the data forever: a column named `telegram_user_id` holding values that are not Telegram ids, with **no way ever to separate the real ones from the invented ones**, and a bot that would try to deliver to them. It converts a schema problem into a data problem, and data problems are the ones that cannot be fixed later. Written down here so nobody proposes it again. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4b: existing learners get fresh ids (1, 2, 3), NOT their Telegram ids seeded into `users.id`.** The tempting shortcut would have made the migration touch no data at all. | **This is the load-bearing decision of the slice.** Seeding `id = telegram_user_id` makes the two numbers equal for exactly the three learners we test with, and different only for the first web user. Any code passing a Telegram id where an internal id belongs would then work in every test, in dev, and in production — and break months later for a Telegram-less person, with no test that could have caught it. With fresh small ids the same mistake fails **immediately and loudly**: a 10-digit value matches no `users.id`, so the query returns nothing or the foreign key re-add aborts. **This was not theoretical.** Rewriting the suite surfaced roughly 490 failures, nearly all of them a foreign-key violation naming the exact table and value — every one a place the old code had conflated identity with an address. Under the seeded scheme all of them would have passed. We paid one data rewrite of small tables to make the type error self-detecting. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4b took migration 011 and every unwritten slice below it shifted by one** (W5 011→012 … W18 018→019), corrected in `docs/TASKS-v3-web.md` in the same commit. | Taking a number above everything claimed — say 019 — would have *worked* on production, because `db.py:171` computes pending as a **set difference and not `v > max`**, so W5's later 011 would still be applied. That is exactly the problem. On a **fresh** database the runner applies in ascending numeric order, so 011 runs *before* 019 there and *after* it on production, and W5's file would have to be correct against two different parent schemas. The schema history stops being replayable, which is the one thing a numbered-file scheme exists to give you. **This is not #49's failure**: #49 was a slice shipping a number the authoritative table did not know about. Here the table was corrected in the same commit and the renumbered slices **have not been written** — nothing on disk, nothing applied, no `schema_version` row moved. Renumbering an applied migration is #49; renumbering a row in a planning table is bookkeeping. W4a made the same distinction in the other direction on 2026-08-24 and declined 011 for a slice that needed no migration. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4b, found while reading the schema and not in the plan: `access_requests.telegram_user_id` was the table's PRIMARY KEY, so it could not be made nullable in place.** `ALTER COLUMN … DROP NOT NULL` on a primary-key column errors. | A syntax-level stop, not a data risk — the migration would have aborted at that statement. The table therefore needed its own ordered re-key: add `id` identity, add and backfill `user_id`, **move the primary key**, and only *then* drop the NOT NULL and add the unique constraint. Order is load-bearing rather than stylistic. Verified by grep that **nothing in the schema references `access_requests`**, so moving its key disturbed no other table — stated explicitly rather than left to silence, because moving a primary key is exactly the kind of thing that discovers a dependent foreign key at execution time. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4b: the `UNIQUE (telegram_user_id)` added to `access_requests` is not cosmetic — without it, `request_access` and `save_onboarding` would have started failing at RUNTIME, after a green migration.** | Both use `ON CONFLICT (telegram_user_id)`, which needs a unique constraint on that column. Losing it in the re-key would not have failed at migration time or in any schema test; it would have failed the first time a stranger tapped **Request access** on a deployed bot. That is the worst failure shape available — green everywhere, broken in production, on a path nobody exercises during a deploy. `tests/test_migration_011.py::test_on_conflict_targets_still_exist` exists to say so. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4b: `is_approved` splits into `is_approved(user_id)` and `is_approved_telegram(telegram_user_id)`, both inside `access_control.py`.** | Two of its five callers — `gate_unapproved` and `/start` — ask about somebody who has **no `users` row at all**, which is precisely what 005 built `access_requests` for. One signature genuinely cannot serve both, and overloading it to accept either is the ambiguity this slice exists to remove. Keeping both in the same module preserves `tests/test_auth.py:1083`'s standing rule that approval is answered in one place and nothing else runs the query. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4b: identity translates in exactly two modules, and a parse test enforces it rather than a convention.** `core/services/identity.py` (with `access_control.py` exempt, for the pre-account reason above) and `apps/bot/identity.py`. | The failure it prevents is **silent**: a Telegram id passed into a service does not raise, it selects zero rows, so a feature stops working for everyone while the suite stays green. That is how free correction (M2) died three times. The rule is precise about what it forbids: **keying a query** on `telegram_user_id` (a WHERE, a JOIN predicate, an ON CONFLICT target) is translation and is confined; *selecting* the column is not — it is a delivery address, read the same way `timezone` is. Logging an id is not translation either. Same instrument as W1's SQL boundary and W4's lemmatiser rule, and it was **proven not inert**: introducing one violation on each side makes each half fail, and reverting makes it pass. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4b: `EligibleUser` and `MotivationUser` carry `id` and `telegram_address` as two fields, and delivery skips a NULL address.** | Before this slice one number was both the learner's identity and their chat id — which is why `chat_id=user_id` read as correct throughout the bot for two years. Keeping them as separate fields makes it impossible for a caller to use one as the other. The skip is correct (this bot cannot reach a web-only learner) and **silent**, which is filed as **#95** rather than left to be rediscovered. |

Feed it an internal id and the comparison can never match — the operator silently loses `/admin`, and `/stats` silently loses its fossil-sweep line, with no error anywhere and no existing test covering it (the tests do not set the variable). The fifth was **inlined rather than going through `_is_operator`**, which is why it nearly survived. Each parameter is now named `telegram_user_id` for the type it takes, so the mistake cannot be made by accident. `_maybe_fanout_shared_book` in `book.py` had the same shape and now translates rather than comparing. |

Adding the *constraint* is a separate act with an asymmetric downside: one unmappable value (an operator id, an account deleted before 006) would abort **the entire identity migration** over a column that is not identity-critical. Expected outcome is clean, but "expected" is not "verified", so the runbook asks first and the human is not making that call at 23:00 mid-deploy. |

On production's handful of rows it would have been invisible; on a dev database carrying 31,105 `access_requests` rows of test residue it took 34 seconds — above the ~30s ceiling the plan set for stopping and re-planning. Comparing keys as TEXT with an explicit `'~NULL~'` sentinel is both correct (a sentinel string cannot collide with a user id) and hashable. **The dev pollution was worth having**: production would never have shown this. |

Every value 011 overwrites is recoverable from a column 011 preserves, so nothing in the reverse falls back to restore-from-dump. **Proven, not asserted:** forward then back produces a byte-identical schema *and* byte-identical data (`pg_dump` compared both ways), and `schema_version` returns to 10. The guard refuses once `users.telegram_user_id IS NULL` **or** `access_requests.telegram_user_id IS NULL` exists — a web-originated request cannot be represented in the old schema either, so the same expiry applies to both tables. After that point the pre-migration dump is the only path. |

That is the bug in three rows, and it is more legible than any prose description of it. **231 of the floor's 2,000 lemmas dropped out of coverage for `7222549221`.** |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4a: demotion is gated on `MAY_LOWER = {review, placement, tapped}`, not on `source_rank` and not on `AUTHORITATIVE_SOURCES`. Rank measures authority; demotion needs evidence of not-knowing; they are different properties.** | W4's rule let any higher-ranked source write any state, including a lower one. `v2_encountered` is rank 1 and `assumption` is rank 0, so a passive exposure demoted an assumed-known word — and **exposure says nothing about not-knowing**. A word appearing in a reading the bot sent is evidence the learner has *met* it; for a top-2000 lemma the frequency band is by far the stronger prior. Three sources do carry the claim: a measured lapse (`review`), a diagnostic result (`placement`), and the learner stopping at the word (`tapped`). **`tapped` is the reason the two sets cannot be collapsed** — it may lower a state but it may not ignore rank, so it is in `MAY_LOWER` and not in `AUTHORITATIVE_SOURCES`. A test asserts the strict superset relationship so a later edit cannot quietly merge them. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4a: the fault was invisible to 1,050 passing tests and surfaced only on production data with real history. That is the argument for reading the harvest numbers rather than merely printing them.** | Every W4 ledger test wrote a state onto a row it had just created. **None of them put an exposure on top of a frequency-floor row** — the one combination the rule got backwards — because the floor and the harvest were tested separately and the interaction between them was the thing neither covered. W4a's regression test is exactly that shape: seed the floor, run a harvest that mentions floor words, assert coverage does not fall. The general lesson is narrower than "test more": a rule about *conflict* needs tests on *pre-existing rows*, not on fresh ones. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4a carries no migration at all, and 011 was not taken.** | `docs/TASKS-v3-web.md`'s authoritative migration table already assigns **011 to W5** (`items`, `item_attempts`, the `errors.source` CHECK widening, the `track_weights` default). Taking it would have renumbered a slice that is already specified. Nothing needed one anyway: the conflict rule lives in a SQL string in `core/services/lexicon.py`, generated from `states.py`, and no column, constraint or index changes. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4a: the repair is a command, not DDL inside a migration, and it is conservative by construction.** `python -m core.lexicon.repair [--dry-run]`. | **Why not a migration:** the affected row set depends on `LEXICON_ASSUMED_KNOWN_TOP_N`, which is configuration, and a migration that read configuration would bake one deployment's setting into the schema history. **Why the filter is exact rather than heuristic:** the floor inserts *every* lemma at or above `top_n`, so a lemma in that band now sitting at `v2_encountered`/`v2_studied` can only be an overwritten floor row — the harvest cannot have inserted it fresh. The `first_seen_at` window is belt-and-braces on top of that, and it matters only if the floor is ever run separately from the harvest. **Why `known/v2_produced` is excluded:** self-produced English is stronger evidence than the floor's hypothesis, and 25 such rows exist for `7222549221` — raising them would be a second bug wearing a repair's clothes. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4a: the rule change alone would not have fixed production, and that is why the repair exists.** | No future write raises those rows back. `v2_encountered` can only ever assert `seen`, so a demoted floor lemma would have stayed demoted until a placement test or a card review happened to touch it — which for 231 lemmas is effectively never. A fixed rule with unrepaired rows is a system that is correct going forward and wrong about its own history. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: the licence gate ran first and the plan's §4 table was wrong in both rows. No code was written until it was corrected.** FrequencyWords is **CC BY-SA 4.0 for content**, not MIT — its README says verbatim *"MIT License for code.<br>CC-by-sa-4.0 for content"*, and the word lists are content. CEFR-J v1.6 is **not Creative Commons at all** — copyright Tono Laboratory at TUFS, *"can be used for both research and commercial purposes with a proper acknowledgement of the source"*. | The two errors point in opposite directions and one of them **voided a fallback the plan was relying on**. CEFR-J is *better* than assumed (no share-alike, commercial use explicit) but is a bespoke permission rather than a licence. FrequencyWords is *worse*: the plan's escape hatch — "frequency bands only, CEFR approximated from rank, no share-alike anywhere" — does not exist, because the frequency half is itself BY-SA. Escaping share-alike would need a different frequency source, not a narrower use of this one. Both raw inputs are recorded in `data/LICENCES.md` with verbatim quotes, URLs, retrieval date and SHA-256. Had the gate not run, the repository would now carry a file labelled MIT that is not. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: `data/lexemes.tsv` and `data/inflections.tsv` are licensed CC BY-SA 4.0, and the attribution is written as comment lines *inside each file*, not only in `data/LICENCES.md`.** | Share-alike does not restrict selling the application and touches no code, but it travels with the two data files wherever they go — and a data file is precisely the thing that gets copied out of a repository on its own. An attribution that lives only in a sibling Markdown file is an attribution that survives exactly as long as nobody moves the data. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: the raw inputs are deliberately NOT committed — only the merged output, the generator and the input checksums.** | This is what keeps the merged file **re-derivable and therefore deletable**. If either licence has to be unwound — at a sale, at an open-sourcing, or on a rights holder's request — removing `data/lexemes.tsv` is a delete plus a re-run of `scripts/build_lexicon.py` against a different source, not a re-architecture. Nothing else in the codebase embeds the data. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: the lemmatiser is a committed lookup table generated offline, not a runtime NLP library.** `scripts/build_lexicon.py` uses `lemminflect` and `openpyxl` on a developer's machine; the server sees neither, and they are pinned in `scripts/requirements-lexicon-build.txt`. | Judged on the four things that matter here. **Deploy:** spaCy needs `spacy download` and NLTK needs `nltk.download('wordnet')` — a new step in a deployment sequence that is settled, on a host that is shared. **Memory:** spaCy is ~100 MB resident × N uvicorn workers. **Determinism:** a spaCy lemma depends on a statistical POS prediction that moves with the model version, so the same word could lemmatise differently after an unrelated upgrade — which for a ledger keyed on lemmas is silent corruption. **Offline:** the production host must not fetch a model at boot. A committed table is byte-identical across versions, inspectable in a diff, and costs zero new runtime dependencies. Irregulars are a closed class (~180 verbs, ~80 plurals) the generator handles, so the accuracy argument for a library largely evaporates. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: `lemminflect` and `openpyxl` are pinned in `scripts/requirements-lexicon-build.txt`, and `requirements.txt` is not touched at all.** | The plan proposed a dev section inside `requirements.txt`. Reading the parser first showed that to be a trap. `test_requirements_mirror_core_dependencies` moves its cursor only on a comment matching `^#\s*(apps/\|packages/\|tests\b)`; a `# dev tooling` header matches none of those, so it neither opens nor closes a section. A dev block placed after the `# packages/core` lines would therefore be collected **as a core dependency** and fail the test, and one placed at the end of the file would pass *only because of where it sits* — until someone reordered the file, at which point it would break with no obvious cause. A separate file the mirror test never reads has no such failure mode, and the mirror test is left exactly as it was. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: authoritative sources — `review` and `placement` — write regardless of rank. Rank orders inferences only.** | Two failures, one at equal rank and one below it, and the second is the expensive one. *Equal:* an FSRS lapse is `review` over `review`, so a rule requiring a strictly higher state discards it and every lapse from W7 onward vanishes. *Lower:* `placement` is rank 6 and `review` is rank 5, so a rank gate would drop **every** review of a word for the month after a placement test — promotions as well as lapses. W7's reviewer would appear to work while changing nothing, which is this project's signature failure. PRD §9's monthly re-test has the same shape from the other side: the one instrument built to measure regression must be able to record one. Between two direct measurements the newer wins; idempotency is carried by the `IS DISTINCT FROM` guard instead of by rank. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: `register` is coalesced in the upsert, never overwritten with NULL.** | Not asked for, and a real bug caught while writing the rule. `register` is in the `SET` clause, so a later equal-rank raise carrying no register signal — a `v2_studied` write moving `seen` to `learning`, say — would have silently erased a `slang` tag W13 had set. `COALESCE(EXCLUDED.register, user_lexemes.register)` on both sides of the guard makes the column additive: a write with nothing to say about register says nothing. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: `lexemes` is growable, with an `origin` column in 010 — and it is named `origin`, not `source`.** A lemma is added at runtime only by `ensure_lexeme`, from a form the learner actually tapped, with `freq_rank`/`freq_band`/`cefr` NULL. `lemmatize` still returns `None` rather than guessing. | A closed 15k list would cap the system's entire vocabulary permanently: `user_lexemes.lexeme_id` is a foreign key, so a lemma with no row could never be recorded, and W13's tap-to-define exists precisely for words *outside* a frequency-ranked list — the tap would have silently done nothing. Resolving and creating are kept as two operations with two callers, which is what stops a suffix rule inventing vocabulary. The column is `origin` because `user_lexemes.source` already means "what evidence wrote this ledger row"; two `source` columns meaning different things in adjacent tables is the S24 `Meaning`/`Translation` trap in another costume. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: NULL `freq_rank` means "rarer than the seed list's tail", not "missing", and the seed never deletes.** | Stated so no later consumer has to guess: coverage does not read rank at all, the frequency floor filters `freq_rank IS NOT NULL` so a grown lemma is never assumed known, and W8's banding must sort NULL last rather than coerce it to zero — a fake band is indistinguishable from a real one. The no-delete rule is the more dangerous half: a "sync" that removed lemmas absent from the file would cascade through `user_lexemes` and destroy ledger history, which is the one thing in this schema that cannot be rebuilt. A re-seed *enriches* a grown row instead, preserving `id` — the thing `user_lexemes` points at — so a word that finally makes the frequency list keeps its history. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: coverage is computed on tokens, and `learning` does not count.** Excluded from numerator and denominator: numerals, ASR disfluency fillers, and proper nouns. Real interjections (`wow`, `oh`, `yeah`) stay in. Contractions are expanded rather than becoming their own lexemes. | **Tokens because** comprehensible input is defined on running words, and because the top hundred types are roughly half of all tokens — a type-based number would measure the text's vocabulary breadth rather than the learner's comprehension and would put W12's band permanently out of reach. **`learning` excluded because** coverage answers "can the learner follow this in real time", and `learning` is exactly the state where they cannot retrieve at speed; counting it would inflate the number and select material they drown in. **Contractions expanded because** a `gonna` row fragments a ledger that already holds `go` and `to`, and W13's tap-to-define wants the expansion anyway. Multiword items are out of scope for coverage: `lexemes.lemma` is `TEXT UNIQUE`, so `'hard pass'` fits later with no migration. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: the proper-noun rule keys on the CEFR tag, not on presence in the lexeme table — and a casing gate runs in front of it.** | The plan's rule was "capitalised, not sentence-initial, lowercase form not in `lexemes`". Measuring the built table showed that cannot work: the frequency list is lowercased, so names sit high in it — `john` at rank 548, `sarah` at 1221, `paris` at 1107 — and "is it in the table" therefore cannot tell a name from a word for exactly the names a transcript is full of. **None of them carries a CEFR tag; `internet` does.** A curated vocabulary syllabus is the discriminator that actually separates the two. The casing gate is the plan's, unchanged: outside a normal capitalisation band the rule is switched off and `CoverageReport.proper_nouns_detected` says so, because on all-uppercase captions the rule would fire on genuinely unknown words and *exclude* them, biasing coverage high — the drowning direction. Switched off, names merely count unknown and the bias is low, which W12 survives. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: the inflection table is consulted *before* the identity check, and homographs resolve by frequency rank.** | Ordering is load-bearing and the first version had it backwards. `saw` earns a lexeme row of its own at rank 10,415 off `sawing`/`sawed`, while `see` sits at rank 49. Identity-first left "I saw a film" resolving to the carpentry tool, so a learner who plainly knows `see` got no credit for it. The builder only writes a table entry when its lemma outranks the form's own entry, so a table hit already means the better-ranked reading won. The cost — the rarer reading of a homograph is not separately reachable — is one coverage does not need, and W13 can revisit it with a real word-sense step. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: lemma frequency is aggregated per lemma, not per surface form, and the surface→lemma choice is made on corpus counts rather than a fixed part-of-speech order.** | The ledger is keyed on lemmas, so `run` must not compete with `running` for a place in the top-N band — between them they are one word. The count rule matters more than it looks: lemminflect offers a base-form noun reading for almost every `-ing` word, so a rule letting the surface win outright gave `run` and `running` separate entries, which is the exact fragmentation the lemma key exists to prevent. Counts settle it the way usage does — `run` outweighs `running` so `running` folds in, while `building` outweighs `build` and keeps its own entry, which is right: a building is a different word from building something. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: the v2 harvest reads only keyboard-authored journal rows — `quiz`, `text`, `reading`, `conversation` — and only `errors.you_said`.** `voice` and `diary` are dropped entirely rather than downgraded to `seen`; a `capture` row is counted and reported as a data defect. | ASR output is not evidence that a learner produces a word. CLAUDE.md §5 bars journaling mishearings but that guard is upstream and imperfect, and a mishearing promoted to `known` is a permanently known word the learner never said — a wrong entry is permanent damage, a missing one is recoverable. Dropping beats downgrading because a `seen` row for a word the recogniser invented is still a false row, and `readings.body` and the chunk sentences already supply the exposure signal honestly. `capture` is someone else's English by definition and is barred from the journal outright, so a `capture` row in `errors` is a pre-existing defect worth surfacing rather than silently harvesting. `correct_form` is never read: it is the bot's own wording. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: `presented_at IS NOT NULL` gates both `chunks.chunk` and `chunks.full_sentence`.** | They are two columns of one row. The plan gated only the first, which would have let a fanned-out chunk that was never shown contribute its sentence as exposure while its target phrase was correctly excluded — the exact inversion of the S25 signal, crediting exposure that provably did not happen. `seen` does not count toward coverage so the numeric damage was small, but it would have read later as intentional. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: the "words you know" figure counts evidenced rows only (`source <> 'assumption'`). Recorded now; W19 builds it.** | PRD §11 promises a real number against streak-day theatre. With the floor at 2,000 a `known` count opens above two thousand before the learner has done anything, and its first movement once a placement test corrected the assumption would be **downward** — precisely what the guilt ban and "raises announced, drops silent" exist to prevent. Two consequences W19 inherits: the six-month target line must be drawn on the same basis it counts, or the line is meaningless; and the counter is deliberately small until the first placement run, at which point `source='placement'` rows *are* evidenced and it jumps to a real measured figure — the "levels change on measurement, never on vibes" moment PRD §9 asks for. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: the empty-ledger problem is solved by both mechanisms — a top-2,000 frequency floor at the weakest rank, plus the v2 harvest — on the human's decision.** | Neither works alone. The harvest alone yields roughly 0% coverage, because the v2 corpus honestly justifies only `seen`/`learning` for most high-frequency words and neither state counts; the floor alone gives every learner the same ledger. `N = 2000` is deliberately below PRD §2.1's B1 estimate of 2,500–3,000, so the error is toward material that is slightly too hard — which a learner can see and say so about — rather than material they drown in silently. Measured consequence, recorded because it is invisible otherwise: proper nouns occupy part of the floor, so the learner is assumed to know rather fewer than 2,000 real words. That biases it conservative, in the safe direction. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: a header row is skipped by *position*, never by matching its text.** | Found by a count that was one short and nothing else. The first parser skipped any row whose first field read `lemma` or `surface` — which silently dropped the English word **`surface`**, rank 1946, B1. No error, one lexeme missing, and a hole in coverage that nothing would ever have pointed at. It is the same shape as the `Meaning`/`Translation` mismatch: a heuristic that reads data as metadata. A test now asserts `surface` survives. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: the promised "no second word-tokenising regex anywhere" test was narrowed to "only `normalize.py` reads the lexicon data files".** | Written as promised, it flagged six innocent patterns — migration filenames in `core/db.py`, slug normalisers in `prep.py` and `books.py`, a CSV-header splitter in `watch_import.py`. Every one is a legitimate tokeniser that has nothing to do with English vocabulary, and exempting them by name would have made the test a rubber stamp — exactly what `test_no_sql_outside_services` refuses to be. What actually drifts is the *parse*, and the narrower test catches it: one module reads `data/lexemes.tsv`, and the identity assertion in `test_lexicon_coverage.py` covers the other half by pinning the seed builder and the coverage path to the same function object. Narrower and enforceable beats broad and exempted. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W4: the Python batch reduction and the SQL conflict rule are asserted equivalent across all 169 source×state pairs.** | One statement may not touch the same row twice, so a batch has to be de-duplicated in Python before it is sent. Reducing by a different rule than the database applies is drift with no symptom — the batch path and the single-write path would disagree only on inputs nobody tested. The test drives every ordered pair through the database and compares the result to `wins()`. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W3: free correction is verified alive in Telegram after the restart.** At **11:49 local Vilnius (08:49 UTC)** — about half an hour after the 08:18–08:20 UTC restart — a plain `how are you` in a private chat drew `👍 Correct word order and natural phrasing for a greeting question.` | This is the check that matters, and it is not interchangeable with a test. M2 has been silently killed three times in this project — by `OpenQuizFilter`, by the book `ConversationHandler`, and by a session-backed filter edge case — and a plain-text message arriving at the bot and coming back corrected is the only check that ever caught any of them. W3 re-pointed the service that handler calls, so dispatch was exactly what was at risk. Together with the `INFO core.services.correction` startup line the loop closes end to end: the taxonomy loaded in the new module, and a real learner message round-tripped through it on the same process. **It proves dispatch, not quality** — the sentence was already fine, so this was the no-error path and the reply is the acknowledgement shape, not a correction with a Murphy reference. #45's single-language rule remains unexercised in the wild on all five paths. |
| 2026-08-24 | **The four remaining Telegram explanation paths (`/diary`, voice, `/capture`, `/talk` close-out) are *deprioritised* by the human — not declined, and not pending.** They stay on the carried-forward list and block nothing. | The three words are not interchangeable and a later reader needs to know which one this was. *Pending* would mean someone is waiting to run them; *declined* would mean they were judged not worth running at all. **Deprioritised** is the actual position: the human's stated reason is that the migration off Telegram is the point, so the bot's surfaces are not worth desk-checking for their own sake. They remain live paths for two learners until W20–W22, which is why they stay on the list rather than leaving it. |
| 2026-08-24 | **Clock convention for this file: server log lines are UTC, Telegram screenshots are local Vilnius (UTC+3). Convert once, write both where it matters, never mix the two in one sentence.** | The W3 closeout put a UTC restart window and a local-time screenshot on the same page, and the difference decides what the evidence means. Worked example, recorded because it reverses the obvious reading: the morning quiz delivered at **09:03 local is 06:03 UTC** — **two hours and fifteen minutes *before* the 08:18–08:20 UTC restart**, not after it. It therefore shows the daily quiz still delivering on production in its expected tap-only shape, and shows **nothing** about the scheduler surviving the restart. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W1c: the unattended off-site backup chain is proven end to end — the cron, the upload and the alarm, with no operator present.** `journalctl -u english-bot`, 08:09:30 UTC: `Off-site backup R2 ok: Newest object english_bot/2026/08/english_bot_2026-08-24_0400.dump is 4.2h old (68436 bytes)`. | Two independent mechanisms agreed without a human between them: the 04:00 cron wrote the dump, and the hourly `backup_freshness` job listed the bucket four hours later and returned `ok`. It ran in `english-bot`, the one process with an operator channel while `english-worker` stays uninstalled (#69), so this is the copy of the alarm that would actually have reached Telegram had it said anything else. 68436 bytes against the 56176 measured by hand on 2026-08-23 is a live database taking overnight writes; a truncated or empty upload would not look like this. W1c's unattended cron therefore moves out of "on the human's report" and into the evidenced group. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W3: the correction extraction is settled by one line of the bot's startup log, `INFO core.services.correction: Loaded 19 error types for correction prompt`, and that argument is the durable part.** | A Python logger name comes from the module's `__name__`, so the prefix names the module that actually executed, not the module it was imported from. `apps/bot/handlers/correction.py` still exists and re-exports the names its eight dependents import — a re-export that had quietly kept its own copy of the taxonomy load would have logged the **old** name. It logged the new one. And `19 error types` says the moved code *ran* and populated the cache in its new location, rather than merely being importable. The whole move is verified from the front end that did not change, without a test asserting it. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W3 deploy: production reports `schema_version: 9`, and that is recorded as confirmation that no migration ran — not as a new fact about 009.** | 009 is W2's and has been applied since the W2 closeout. W3 added a route and moved a service; it carried no `.sql` file. An unchanged version number after a restart is the cheapest available proof that the deploy did what the slice said it would and nothing more. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W3 closeout: `docs/ARCHITECTURE-v3-web.md` is left uncorrected here, and the mismatch is filed as #82 instead.** | Correcting it means enumerating what is actually in `packages/core/services/` today, which is W4's first act anyway. Doing it now from a prompt rather than from the tree would produce a third description written from intention — and intention-written documents are exactly the pattern #82 names, after #77 was the first occurrence. The consequence that made it worth filing rather than deferring silently: W4 is a PLAN slice that reasons about `core/services/` directly, so it must read the repo tree, not §3/§4 of the architecture document. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W3: the correction logic moved to `core/services/correction.py`; it was not in `core/services/` and it was not reimplemented.** The bot handler keeps dispatch, access control, the typing indicator, its retry-with-a-visible-message loop and all `texts.*` rendering, and re-exports the moved names so its eight dependents are untouched. | The slice prompt assumed `core/services/` already held it; it did not — the whole correction path lived in `apps/bot/handlers/correction.py`. That left exactly two options, because `apps/api` may not import `apps.bot` (`test_api_does_not_import_the_worker_job_table`): move it, or write a second correction path. Reimplementing the feature that died silently three times in v2 is the worst idea available, so it moved. The move is behaviour-preserving by construction — `_handle_model_result` now renders what `apply_result` decided instead of re-deriving the cap, the unknown-type drop and the write. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W3 follow-up, before push: `call_model(user, text)` extracted into `core/services/correction.py`; the bot and the route now share the one call site, and `apps/bot/handlers/correction.py` no longer imports `chat` at all.** The four `patch("apps.bot.handlers.correction.chat")` targets were repointed to `core.services.correction.chat`. The retry — including `texts.LLM_RETRY` — stayed in the bot. | **Correcting the record: the first version of this slice shipped two call sites, and the stated reason was wrong.** Both `core.services.correction.correct` and the bot's `_call_llm_with_handler_retry` built the prompt and called `chat` — four duplicated lines including `json_mode=True` and the message role. Nothing would have caught a drift: each front end's tests patch its own module and neither can see the other. **What actually kept the call in the bot was that four tests patched it there** — a test-shaped constraint driving a production decision, which is the wrong way round. The Telegram-coupled retry is a real constraint but a narrower one: it justifies the bot owning the *retry*, because `LLM_RETRY` goes to the learner between attempts and a service has no channel to say so, and it never justified the bot owning the *call*. The guard is structural rather than comparative: the bot cannot name `chat`, and a test pins the **number** of call sites — a test comparing the two argument lists would have gone green on a change made wrongly in both places. Verified by feeding the detector a reintroduced call site, and re-ran the correction real API call afterwards (one, not five: the other four templates were untouched). |
| 2026-08-24 | **W3: `POST /correct` is JSON-only and answers 415 to anything else, enforced by its own dependency rather than inherited from pydantic.** | W2 recorded that `SameSite=Lax` is not the CSRF barrier — the barrier is that every state-changing route is a JSON `POST`, which is never a simple request and therefore always preflights, and the preflight is answered only for the two allowed origins. `/correct` is the first route that actually writes, so the position stopped being theoretical. FastAPI would already answer 422 to a form body against a JSON model, but for an incidental reason that disappears the moment someone adds a `Form(...)` parameter; an explicit guard survives that. Accepting `application/x-www-form-urlencoded` would make this a simple request, remove the preflight, and let a cross-site auto-submitted form reach the route with the learner's cookie. Two tests assert the refusal, and one asserts `application/json; charset=utf-8` is still accepted — a guard that rejected the charset would break real clients while blocking nothing. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W3: `/correct` is rate limited at 30 per client per hour and 120 globally per hour.** | It is the first route that spends money per request, on a surface the production access log shows is scanned continuously. A learner writing every two minutes for a solid hour would use 30; past that it is not practice. A session is required, so the realistic threat is one stolen session rather than a stranger — a stranger has no session at all — which is why the per-client number is the meaningful one and the global ceiling only bounds the blast radius. The machinery (`auth_rate_limits`, `deps.rate_limit`) already existed from W2, so this cost one line. |
| 2026-08-24 | **W3: known issue #45 closed. The single-language / no-transliteration rule is one constant in `core/prompt_rules.py`, appended to all five explanation paths.** Verified live: with fallback on, a Farsi learner's `present_perfect` explanation came back in Persian script (`با since برای نشان دادن شروع از حال کامل استفاده کنید`) and a Lithuanian learner's in Latin — each leaving only the English grammar term in English, which is exactly what the rule's third sentence permits. | **Only the single-language sentences are shared, not the whole explanation-language rule.** The five features do genuinely different things — corrections quote a wrong fragment, capture glosses an opaque phrase in 25 words — and their surrounding wording differs for real reasons. | 2026-08-24 | **W2 closeout: the false-banner diagnosis was wrong, and the way it was wrong is the finding.** W2a traced `main` and concluded the banner could only render after a tap, most likely during the `status=203/EXEC` window. **The production access log refutes both halves.** At 20:26 and 20:30 UTC — the timestamps on both screenshots — there is **no request from the phone's address at all**; `88.118.79.52` first appears at 20:31:05. The banner rendered on a screen whose request never reached the server. And the `203/EXEC` window was **20:08–20:11**, fifteen minutes earlier, by which time `/health` was answering `200` over public HTTPS. | **The trace was correct about the code and irrelevant to the incident.** It read `main`; the phone was running a **service-worker-cached bundle from before the redeploy** — different code entirely. "The banner can only render after a tap" is true of the current build and says nothing about what was cached then. **Cause, high confidence but not fully established:** the old bundle had no `NEXT_PUBLIC_API_URL` inlined, fell back to `http://localhost:8000`, and Safari blocked it as **mixed content on a secure page** — which happens in the browser, before any egress, and therefore leaves no server-side trace. That fits every observed fact: the banner, the absent log lines, both sightings, and its disappearance after a cache-free redeploy plus a home-screen reinstall. **What cannot be confirmed:** whether the old build's message named a different constant than the request used. That bundle is gone, so this stays a conclusion with a caveat, not a proven chain. **The transferable lesson, and the reason this is recorded at all: a trace of current source cannot explain a symptom observed against a cached bundle.** Any future frontend investigation establishes *which build the user was actually running* before it reads a line of code. A PWA with `skipWaiting` makes this the normal case, not an edge one. |

The `GET /health/auth` `200` immediately after is the session resolving, which is the half a registration test cannot prove on its own. A nullable return type cannot express the difference between that and "the request failed", so the difference was lost at the only place left to notice it: a `catch`. `RequireSession` did exactly that, sending both the anonymous visitor and an unreachable API to `/sign-in` — the right destination for one of them, by accident, and the wrong advice for the other, since signing in is precisely what cannot work while the API is down. The first is blast radius: the box carries another team's production service and the only copy of the error journal. The second is that a slice able to satisfy its own server criteria would mark them met — and the W2 report already had to distinguish "written" from "run" three times. The server's `.env` holds five real `R2_*` keys with Object Read & Write on the bucket that holds **every off-site copy of the error journal**, and `pytest -q` sits in the verification path — so the same leak on a different machine does not fail harmlessly, it authenticates. The failure was also a rule-3 defect in its own right — `monkeypatch.delenv` followed by `load_settings()` never exercised the default at all, because `load_dotenv` put the value back from the repo `.env`, so a green run meant different things on different machines. | 2026-08-23 | **W2: Better Auth rejected; auth implemented in FastAPI with `py_webauthn`.** `docs/ARCHITECTURE-v3-web.md` §2's Auth row and §6's `POST /auth/* Better Auth` line **were wrong and have been corrected in this slice** (§6 now lists all ten routes). | Better Auth is a TypeScript library that runs in Next.js route handlers and needs a direct PostgreSQL connection. `apps/web` is on Vercel; PostgreSQL binds `127.0.0.1`. The three bridges were all worse: exposing the journal's database to the public internet to solve a login problem (to an egress range Vercel does not pin); a Node auth service on the box — a third process and a second language for one job, close enough to CLAUDE.md §2's microservice ban to avoid; or Clerk, the vendor Better Auth was chosen to avoid. FastAPI adds no process, no vendor and no open port, and keeps `route → service → SQL` as the single spine. Recorded as a deliberate departure so the document does not sit contradicting the code. |

The 009 schema accommodates magic link arriving later **without a second `ALTER TABLE users`**. |

If typing an existing address were sufficient, whoever typed it first would own the account and the error journal behind it. Adding a second passkey to an already-enrolled account is a different path requiring an existing session, never a token. |
